Global smooth solutions to 4D quasilinear wave equations with short pulse initial data
Ding Bingbing, Xin Zhouping, Yin Huicheng

Abstract
. In this paper, we establish the global existence of smooth solutions to general 4D quasilinear wave equations satisfying the first null condition with the short pulse initial data. Although the global existence of small data solutions to 4D quasilinear wave equations holds true without any requirement of null conditions, yet for short pulse data, in general, it is sufficient and necessary to require the fulfillment of the first null condition to have global smooth solutions. It is noted that short pulse data are extensions of a class of spherically symmetric data, for which the smallness restrictions are imposed on angular directions and along the outgoing directional derivative ∂ t + ∂ r \partial_{t}+\partial_{r} , but the largeness is kept for the incoming directional derivative ∂ t − ∂ r \partial_{t}-\partial_{r} . We expect that here methods can be applied to study the global smooth solution or blowup problem with short pulse initial data for the general 2D and 3D quasilinear wave equations when the corresponding null conditions hold or not. On the other hand, as some direct applications of our main results, one can show that for the short pulse initial data, the smooth solutions to the 4D irrotational compressible Euler equations for Chaplygin gases, 4D nonlinear membrane equations and 4D relativistic membrane equations exist globally since their nonlinearities satisfy the first null condition; while the smooth solutions to the 4D irrotational compressible Euler equations for polytropic gases generally blow up in finite time since the corresponding first null condition does not hold.

Keywords:
Quasilinear wave equations, short pulse initial data, the first null condition,

inverse foliation density, Goursat problem, global existence

Mathematical Subject Classification:
35L05, 35L72

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Formulation of the problem and main results

Consider general
n-
D quasilinear wave equations of the form:

\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{n}g^{\alpha\beta}(\phi,\partial\phi)\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\phi=0,

(1.1)

where
n\geq 2
,
(x^{0},x)=(t,x^{1},\cdots,x^{n})\in[1,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\partial=(\partial_{0},\partial_{1},\cdots,\partial_{n})=(\partial_{x^{0}},\partial_{x^{1}},\cdots,\partial_{x^{n}})
,

g^{\alpha\beta}(\phi,\partial\phi)=g^{\beta\alpha}(\phi,\partial\phi)
are smooth
functions of their arguments.
In addition, without loss of generality, it is assumed that for small
(\phi,\partial\phi)
,

g^{\alpha\beta}(\phi,\partial\phi)=m^{\alpha\beta}+g^{\alpha\beta,0}\phi+\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma=0}^{n}\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\phi+h^{\alpha\beta}(\phi,\partial\phi)

(1.2)

with
m^{00}=-1
,
m^{ii}=1
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
m^{\alpha\beta}=0
for
\alpha\neq\beta
,
g^{\alpha\beta,0}

and
\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}
being constants, and
h^{\alpha\beta}(0,0)=\nabla_{(\phi,\partial\phi)}h^{\alpha\beta}(0,0)=0
.

If (
1.1
) is equipped with the small initial data

\phi(1,x)=\delta\varphi_{0}(x),\partial_{t}\phi(1,x)=\delta\varphi_{1}(x),

(1.3)

where
\delta>0
is sufficiently small,
(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})(x)\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\text{supp$\varphi_{0}$, supp$\varphi_{1}$}\subset B(0,M)
and
M>0
is a constant,
then there exist extensive literatures on the global existence of smooth solution to (
1.1
)-(
1.3
).
Indeed, for
n\geq 5
, or
n=4
but
g^{\alpha\beta,0}=0
for all
0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 4
,
the global existence of smooth solution
\phi
to (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) is established
and meanwhile
|\partial\phi|\leq C\delta(1+t)^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}
is obtained in
[
25
]
(also see Chapter 6 of
[
20
]
or
[
28
]
).
The key points of the analyses are based on
the standard energy estimates for wave equations, the smallness of
\|\phi(t,x)\|_{H_{x}^{2[\frac{n}{2}]+2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

and the following Klainerman-Sobolev inequality:

|\partial\phi(t,x)|\leq\frac{C}{(1+|t-r|)^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+t)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}}\displaystyle\sum_{|I|\leq[\frac{n}{2}]+2}\|Z^{I}\partial\phi(t,x)\|_{L_{x}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

where
Z\in\{\partial,x^{i}\partial_{j}-x^{j}\partial_{i},x^{i}\partial_{t}+t\partial_{i},1\leq i,j\leq n,\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}x^{k}\partial_{k}\}
and
r=|x|=\sqrt{(x^{1})^{2}+\cdot\cdot\cdot+(x^{n})^{2}}
.

For
n=4
and
g^{\alpha\beta,0}\not=0
for some
(\alpha,\beta)
, the global existence of the solution
\phi
with

|\phi|\leq C\delta(1+t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and
|\partial\phi|\leq C\delta(1+t)^{-\frac{3}{2}}
is shown in
[
29
]
,
where the smallness of
\|\phi(t,x)\|_{H_{x}^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}

and the following Hardy-type inequality for
\text{supp${}_{x}\phi(t,x)$}\subset\{x:|x|\leq M+t-1\}

\bigl\|\frac{\phi(t,x)}{1+|t-r|}\bigr\|_{L_{x}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\leq C\|\partial\phi(t,x)\|_{L_{x}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}

(1.4)

are crucial.

When
n=3
, it follows from

[
8
,
24
,
30
,
4
,
31
,
14
]

that there exists a global smooth solution
\phi

with
|\phi|\leq C\delta(1+t)^{-1}(1+|r-t|)
and
|\partial\phi|\leq C\delta(1+t)^{-1}
if the first null condition holds (namely,
\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma=0}^{3}\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\xi_{\alpha}\xi_{\beta}\xi_{\gamma}\equiv 0
for
\xi_{0}=-1

and
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
); otherwise, if the first null condition fails, then there
exists a solution
\phi
to (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) which blows up in finite time,
one can see
[
3
,
13
,
15
,
22
,
23
]
.

When
n=2
and the coefficients
g^{\alpha\beta}

are independent of
\phi
(i.e.,
\displaystyle g^{\alpha\beta}(\phi,\partial\phi)=g^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)
),
and one writes that for small
\partial\phi
,

\displaystyle g^{\alpha\beta}(\partial\phi)=m^{\alpha\beta}+\sum_{\gamma=0}^{2}\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\phi+\sum_{\gamma,\nu=0}^{2}h^{\alpha\beta,\gamma\nu}\partial_{\gamma}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi+O(|\partial\phi|^{3}),

(1.5)

where
h^{\alpha\beta,\gamma\nu}
are constants, then it is shown in
[
2
]
that the problem (
1.1
)
with (
1.3
)
has a global smooth solution
\phi
satisfying
|\partial\phi|\leq C\delta(1+t)^{-1/2}
if both the first and second null conditions hold (that is,

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma=0}^{2}\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\xi_{\alpha}\xi_{\beta}\xi_{\gamma}\equiv 0
and
\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\nu=0}^{2}h^{\alpha\beta,\gamma\nu}\xi_{\alpha}\xi_{\beta}

\xi_{\gamma}\xi_{\nu}\equiv 0
for
\xi_{0}=-1
and
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
).
Furthermore, if either the first or second null condition fails, there exists a
solution
\phi
to (
1.1
)
and (
1.3
) which blows up in finite time, one can see
[
1
]
and
[
3
]
.

Generally speaking, for the global existence or blowup of smooth
small solutions to nonlinear wave equations, it is more difficult to study the 2D and 3D cases compared with
the 4D or higher multidimensional cases
since the solutions to the corresponding linear wave equations admit slower time-decay rates in 2D and 3D cases.
We now briefly review the proof procedures for the
n-
D (
n=2,3
) cases of (
1.1
) in the references mentioned above.
Under the null conditions, the proofs of global small data solution
\phi

are based on the smallness of
\partial^{\alpha}\phi
(
|\alpha|\leq N_{0}
with
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
N_{0}
being appropriately large),
suitably weighted energy estimates for wave equations, Klainerman-Sobolev inequality, Hardy-type inequality (
1.4
), and
the following important estimates:

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle|{\tilde{g}}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}u\partial_{\gamma}v|\leq|Y\partial u||\partial v|+|\partial^{2}u||Yv|,\\
\displaystyle|h^{\alpha\beta,\mu\nu}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}u\partial_{\mu}v\partial_{\nu}w|\leq|Y\partial u||\partial v||\partial w|+|\partial^{2}u||Yv||\partial w|+|\partial^{2}u||\partial v||Yw|,\\
\end{array}

where
Y
stands for the one of “good derivatives”

\{Y_{i}=\partial_{i}+\omega_{i}\partial_{t}:\omega_{i}=\frac{x^{i}}{r},i=1,...,n\}
since
Y\phi
admits a better time-decay rate
than that of
\partial\phi
. When the null conditions fail, in order to establish the blowup
of
\phi
(on the blowup time,
\phi
and
\partial\phi
still remain to be small
but
\partial^{2}\phi
blows up), near the light conic surface
\{t=r-M+1:t\geq 1\}
, in the domain

P_{\delta_{0}}=\{(t,r):t+M-1-\delta_{0}\leq r\leq t+M-1,t\geq 1\}
(
\delta_{0}>0
is a suitably fixed constant) one sets

\begin{array}[]{l}\phi(t,x)=\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{r^{\frac{n-1}{2}}}G(\sigma,\omega,\tau),\end{array}

(1.6)

where
\sigma=r-t
,
\omega=\frac{x}{r}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, the slow time variable
\tau=\delta\sqrt{t}
for
n=2
and
\tau=\delta\ln t
for
n=3
.
Substituting (
1.6
) into (
1.1
)
yields such a generalized
n-
D (
n=2,3
) Burgers equation for
\partial_{\sigma}G

\begin{array}[]{l}2^{n-2}\partial^{2}_{\sigma\tau}G+\big(\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{n}\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,0}\omega_{\alpha}\omega_{\beta}G+\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma=0}^{n}\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\omega_{\alpha}\omega_{\beta}\omega_{\gamma}\partial_{\sigma}G\big)\partial^{2}_{\sigma}G\\
=\text{higher order error terms including $\nabla_{\sigma,\tau,\omega}G,\partial_{\omega}^{2}G,\partial_{\tau}^{2}G,\partial_{\tau\omega}^{2}G,\partial_{\sigma\omega}^{2}G,\partial_{\sigma\tau}^{2}G$
of $\delta$}.\end{array}

(1.7)

Then the blowup of
\nabla^{2}_{\sigma,\tau}G
with the lifespan
T_{\delta}
can be established by the geometric blowup method
together with the Nash-Moser-Hörmander iteration which is utilized to treat the free boundary
t=T_{\delta}
. Meanwhile,
in the interior domain

Q_{\delta_{0}}=\{(t,r):r\leq t+M-1-\delta_{0},t\geq 1\}
of the light cone
\{r\leq t+M-1,t\geq 1\}
,
it is shown that
\phi
exists smoothly before
T_{\delta}+1
.

On the other hand, if (
1.1
) is equipped with the general smooth initial data of the form

\phi(1,x)=\varphi_{0}(x),\partial_{t}\phi(1,x)=\varphi_{1}(x),

(1.8)

and the strict hyperbolicity of (
1.1
) is assumed
(in this case, (
1.1
) is demanded to have some special nonlinear structures), the smooth
large solution
\phi
generally blows
up in finite time (see
[
37
]
).

In general, in order to guarantee the strict hyperbolicity of (
1.1
),
the smallness of
(\phi,\partial\phi)
seems to be needed, otherwise, the local well-posedness of the problem (
1.1
)
with (
1.8
) is not expected. This indicates that it is plausible to look for global smooth
solutions to (
1.1
) with initial data (
1.8
) which are large in the sense that

(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})(x)
are small but
\partial^{2}\phi(1,x)
may be large. This motivates one to study
the global existence of smooth solutions to (
1.1
) with the
the following short pulse initial data introduced first by D.Christodoulou
[
10
]
:

\displaystyle\phi|_{t=1}=\delta^{2-\varepsilon}\phi_{0}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega),\ \partial_{t}\phi|_{t=1}=\delta^{1-\varepsilon}\phi_{1}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega),

(1.9)

where
\delta>0
is a small constant,
\varepsilon
is some fixed constant satisfying
0<\varepsilon<1
,

(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})(s,\omega)
are smooth functions of their arguments. By the way,
only the case of
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{2}
in (
1.9
) is considered in
[
10
]
.

This leads to two natural questions:

Problem (A)
Can one show the global existence of smooth solutions to
(
1.1
) with (
1.9
) under the corresponding null conditions?

Problem (B)
Are those null conditions in (A) the sufficient and necessary conditions
for the global existence?

To answer Problems (A) and (B), especially for the more difficult cases of
n=2
and
n=3

in (
1.1
), as the first step, we now investigate the
higher dimensional case of (
1.1
) with
n=4
since
a solution
v
to the 4D free wave equation
\Box v=0
with

(v,\partial_{t}v)(0,x)=(v_{0},v_{1})(x)

\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
has a higher
time-decay rate of
|\partial v|\leq C(1+t)^{-\frac{3}{2}}
, which may make it relatively easier to analyze the solutions.
Meanwhile, it is expected that
the ideas developed here may share lights on the general 2D and 3D cases of (
1.1
) with
(
1.9
) when the corresponding null conditions hold.

Thus, in this paper, we study the 4D quasilinear wave equation

\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{4}g^{\alpha\beta}(\phi,\partial\phi)\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\phi=0

(1.10)

with the short pulse initial data

\displaystyle\phi|_{t=1}=\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}/3}\phi_{0}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega),\ \partial_{t}\phi|_{t=1}=\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}/3}\phi_{1}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega),

(1.11)

where
0<\varepsilon_{0}\leq\frac{7}{144}
is a fixed constant,
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})(s,\omega)
are smooth functions defined in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}

with compact support in
(-1,0)
for the variable
s
. In addition, the following outgoing constraints are imposed:

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r})^{k}\Omega^{l}\partial^{q}\phi|_{t=1}=O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|}),\ 0\leq k\leq 2,

(1.12)

where
\Omega\in\{x^{i}\partial_{j}-x^{j}\partial_{i}:1\leq i<j\leq 4\}
stands for one of the rotational derivatives on
\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
It will be shown that (
1.12
) together with (
1.11
) can guarantee the smallness of
(\phi,\partial\phi)
for all
t\geq 1
.

It follows from (
1.11
)-(
1.12
) that
the short pulse data can be regarded as some suitable extensions of a class of “large” symmetric data, for
which the smallness restrictions are imposed initially on the variations along angular directions and along the “good” direction tangent
to outgoing light conic surface
\{t=r\}
, but the largeness is kept at least for the second order “bad” directional derivatives
of
\partial_{t}-\partial_{r}
. Such short pulse data actually provide a powerful framework to study effectively the blowup or the global existence of
smooth solutions to the multi-dimensional hyperbolic systems or the second order quasilinear wave equations
by virtue of the corresponding knowledge from the 1D cases. We illustrate that the problems of global existence
of solutions, (
1.1
) and (
1.3
), (
1.10
) and (
1.11
)-(
1.12
), correspond essentially
to the case of outgoing waves. Indeed, it is interesting to note that for (
1.1
) and (
1.3
), the
small solution admits naturally a better time decay property along
\partial_{t}+\partial_{r}
than along
\partial_{t}-\partial_{r}
near the light conic surface
\{t=r-M+1:t\geq 1\}

due to
(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r})^{k}\phi=O(t^{-\frac{n-1}{2}(1+k)})

and
(\partial_{t}-\partial_{r})^{k}\phi=O(t^{-\frac{n-1}{2}})
(
k\geq 1,n\geq 2
).
For the large data solution to (
1.10
) with (
1.11
)-(
1.12
), besides the higher order time-decay
rate along
\partial_{t}+\partial_{r}
near the light conic surface as in (
1.1
) with (
1.3
), it can be
derived that
(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r})^{k}\phi
(
0\leq k\leq 2
) admits
a higher order smallness
O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}})
, which may be propagated from the outgoing constraint condition (
1.12
).

The short pulse data chosen in (
1.11
) originate from the monumental work
[
10
]
which reveals the evolutionary formation
of trapped surfaces in the Einstein vacuum space-times, and subsequently was generalized
in
[
26
]
by enlarging the admissible set of initial conditions.
For the Cauchy problem of the 3D quasilinear wave equation

-(1+3G^{\prime\prime}(0)(\partial_{t}u)^{2})\partial_{t}^{2}u+\Delta u=0
with the short pulse initial data

(u,\partial_{t}u)(-2,x)=\big(\delta^{\frac{3}{2}}u_{0}(\frac{r-2}{\delta},\omega),\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{1}(\frac{r-2}{\delta},\omega)\big)
, where
G^{\prime\prime}(0)\not=0
is a constant,
t\geq-2
,

(u_{0},u_{1})(s,\omega)\in C_{0}^{\infty}\left((0,1]\times\mathbb{S}^{2}\right)
,
it is shown in
[
36
]
that the smooth solution
u

will blow up before
t=-1
and further the shock is formed due to the compression of the incoming waves under some assumptions on
(u_{0},u_{1})
.
It should be noted that there exist some global existence results with the short pulse initial data for semilinear wave equations.
For examples, the authors in
[
35
]
study the 3-D semilinear wave equation systems

\Box\varphi^{I}=\displaystyle\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}0\leq\alpha,\beta\leq 3\\
1\leq J,K\leq N\end{array}}A_{JK}^{\alpha\beta,I}\partial_{\alpha}\varphi^{J}\partial_{\beta}\varphi^{K}
(
I=1,...,N
)
with

(\varphi^{I},\partial_{t}\varphi^{I})|_{t=1}=(\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi_{0}^{I}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega),\delta^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi_{1}^{I}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega))
,
where
A_{JK}^{\alpha\beta,I}
are constants,
(\varphi_{0}^{I},\varphi_{1}^{I})(s,\omega)\in C_{0}^{\infty}((-1,0)\times\mathbb{S}^{2})
,
the quadratic nonlinear forms satisfy the corresponding first null condition.
Moreover, it is further assumed that

(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r})^{k}\not{\nabla}^{p}\partial^{q}\varphi^{I}|_{t=1}=O(\delta^{1/2-|q|}),\quad k\leq N_{0},

(1.13)

where
\not{\nabla}
stands for the one of the derivatives on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
,
and
N_{0}\geq 40
is a sufficiently large integer.
Also recently, by some similar ideas of
[
35
]
, the authors in
[
41
]
proved the global existence of the relativistic membrane equation

\partial_{t}(\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{t}\varphi}{\sqrt{1-(\partial_{t}\varphi)^{2}+|\nabla\varphi|^{2}}})-\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{i}(\frac{\partial_{i}\varphi}{\sqrt{1-(\partial_{t}\varphi)^{2}+|\nabla\varphi|^{2}}})=0\quad(n=2,3)

(1.14)

with the short pulse initial data satisfying

(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r})^{k}\not{\nabla}^{l}\partial^{m}\varphi|_{t=1}=O(\delta^{1-m}),\quad 1\leq k\leq N_{0}.

(1.15)

Here it should be pointed out that the special structure of (
1.14
) (including the divergence form and cubic nonlinearity),
the largeness of
N_{0}
and the higher order smallness of
\delta
in (
1.15
) play a crucial role on
the direct energy method in
[
41
]
(in this case, the optical function
t-r
for the linear wave equation can be
utilized, which leads to some suitably modified Lorentzian vector fields). However, this approach fails to apply to the general
case here since our optical function
satisfies the nonlinear eikonal equation of (
1.10
), for which the geometry of corresponding characteristic
surface is crucial.

As explained in
[
16
]
and
[
32
]
,
the constraint condition (
1.12
) is generally over-determined for
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
.
Indeed, if the short pulse initial data (
1.11
) is given, then all the derivatives
\partial^{\alpha}\phi|_{t=1}=O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}/3-|\alpha|})
are
derived. This means
(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r})^{k}\Omega^{l}\partial^{q}\phi|_{t=1}=O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}/3-k-|q|})
for
0\leq k\leq 2
.
However, the orders of
(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r})^{k}\Omega^{l}\partial^{q}\phi|_{t=1}
in (
1.12
) are
O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|})
rather than

O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}/3-k-|q|})
.
Thus, the choices of
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
are not arbitrary in general.
Thanks to the null condition in the quasilinear equation (
1.10
),
(
1.12
) can be fulfilled by the suitable choice of
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
through solving the local short pulse data
problem of (
1.10
).

The first null condition for the equation (
1.10
) is

\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma=0}^{4}\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\xi_{\alpha}\xi_{\beta}\xi_{\gamma}\equiv 0\ \text{for $\xi_{0}=-1$ and
$(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4})\in\mathbb{S}^{3}$}.

(1.16)

In addition, without loss of generality and for simplicity, one can assume that

g^{00}(\phi,\partial\phi)=-1.

(1.17)

Our main result in the paper is stated as

Theorem 1.1
.

Consider the Cauchy problem for the 4D quasilinear wave equation (
1.10
)
with the short pulse initial data (
1.11
) satisfying (
1.12
). Assume that
(
1.17
) holds and the first null condition (
1.16
) is satisfied. Then for
\delta>0
suitably
small, then there exists a global smooth solution
\phi
to the problem (
1.10
)-(
1.11
) such that

\phi\in C^{\infty}([1,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{4})\quad\text{and}\ |\phi|\leq C\delta^{(4-5\varepsilon_{0})/3}t^{-1/2},\ |\partial\phi|\leq C\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}t^{-3/2}

(1.18)

for all time
t\geq 1
, where
C>0
is a uniform constant independent of
\delta
and
\varepsilon_{0}
.

Remark 1.1
.

Note that
\partial^{2}\phi|_{t=1}=O(\delta^{-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{3}})
is large for (
1.11
). Then the global well-posedness
theory of (
1.10
) with (
1.11
)
is totally different from the usual small data solution problem of (
1.10
) with (
1.3
), where the smallness of
\|\phi(1,x)\|_{H_{x}^{6}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}+\|\partial_{t}\phi(1,x)\|_{H_{x}^{5}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}
is required for the latter (see Chapter 6-7 of
[
20
]
).

Remark 1.2
.

It is pointed out that the scope of
0<\varepsilon_{0}\leq\frac{7}{144}
in Theorem
1.1
is not optimal.
For the optimal scope of
\varepsilon_{0}
for the general second order quasilinear wave equations with the related null conditions
and short pulse initial data, one is referred to
[
16
]
and
[
32
]
.

Remark 1.3
.

It follows from
the expression of the solution
v
to the 4D linear wave equation
\Box v=0
with
(v,\partial_{t}v)(0,x)=(v_{0},v_{1})(x)
that both
v
and
\partial v
are generally large
when
\partial_{x}^{2}v_{0}

or
\partial_{x}v_{1}
are large. Note that
(\partial^{2}\phi|_{t=1},\partial(\partial_{t}\phi)|_{t=1})

are large for the initial data (
1.11
) with sufficiently small
\delta>0
. Therefore, the strict hyperbolicity of (
1.10
) is generally lost
since the smallness of
(\phi,\partial\phi)
may be violated. Thus,
it is necessary to impose some constraint conditions like (
1.12
) so that the smallness of
(\phi,\partial\phi)
can be kept
with the development of time (see (
1.18
) of Theorem
1.1
).

Remark 1.4
.

For the 4D quasilinear wave equation (
1.10
)
with
(\phi(1,x),\partial_{t}\phi(1,x))\in(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{4}),

H^{s-1}

(\mathbb{R}^{4}))
and
s>\frac{7}{2}
, the authors in
[
38
]

have established the well-posedness of local solution
\phi\in C([1,T],

H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{4}))\cap C^{1}([1,T],H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{4}))

with some
T>1

(see the equation (1.1) with (1.2) and Theorem 1.2 in
[
38
]
).
If
s>4
, the local well-posedness of (
1.10
) is well known (see
[
20
]
or
[
23
]
).
But for the data
(\phi(1,x),

\partial_{t}\phi(1,x))

in (
1.11
),
\|\phi(1,x)\|_{H^{s}}=O(\delta^{\frac{5}{2}-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4}-s})\to\infty
holds true
when
\delta\to 0+
and
\frac{7}{2}<s\leq 4
.
Therefore, the local well-posedness of smooth solution
\phi
to (
1.10
) in any fixed interval
[1,T]

with
T>1
independent of
\delta

does not follow
[
38
]
. Thanks to the special structures
of the short pulse initial data (
1.11
) with (
1.12
), based on the first null condition (
1.16
),
both the local and global existence of
\phi
will be established.

Remark 1.5
.

For second order multidimensional quasilinear wave equations
or multidimensional quasilinear hyperbolic systems with the genuinely nonlinear structures, the formation of shocks has been studied (see
[
11
]
,
[
19
]
,
[
33
]
,
[
36
]
, and
[
39
]
-
[
40
]
)
by the remarkable ideas in
[
9
]
. In the breaking-through work
[
9
]
,
with the help of differential geometry, D. Christodoulou constructed the “inverse foliation density”
\mu
which
measures the compression of the outgoing characteristic surfaces, and showed the formation of shocks
for 3-D relativistic Euler equations
under the smallness assumptions on the initial data. In this process,
D. Christodoulou proved that
\mu
is positive away from the shock
but approaches
0
near the blowup curve (
\mu\rightarrow 0+
corresponds to the intersection of
outgoing characteristic surfaces).
Motivated by the crucial differential geometric methods in
[
9
,
10
,
36
,
39
]
, in the present paper, on the contrary, we will show
\mu\geq C>0
for all time
t\geq 1

and derive the global energy estimates near the outermost outgoing conic surface
\{t=r\}
for problem (
1.10
)-(
1.11
)
with (
1.12
).
This leads to that near
\{t=r\}
, the characteristic conic surfaces never intersect
and the solution of (
1.10
)-(
1.11
) exists globally.

Remark 1.6
.

It is emphasized that if the null condition (
1.16
) does not hold,
then the solution
\phi
of (
1.10
) may blow up in finite time whether or not (
1.12
) is fulfilled.
In fact,
for the 4D quasilinear wave equation
(1+\partial_{t}\phi)\partial_{t}^{2}\phi-\Delta\phi=0

with the initial data
(\phi|_{t=1},\partial_{t}\phi|_{t=1})=(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}/3}\phi_{0}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega),\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}/3}\phi_{1}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega))
and
0<\varepsilon_{0}<3

(obviously, the corresponding null condition is not fulfilled), by the completely analogous arguments as in
[
36
]
,

[
16
]
and
[
32
]
,
one can prove that the solution
\phi
blows up before the time
1+O(\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}/3})
and further the shock is formed under
the assumption of
\partial_{s}\phi_{1}(s_{0},\omega_{0})\geq 2
at some point
(s_{0},\omega_{0})\in(0,1]\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
This indicates that although the global small data solution of (
1.10
) exists for generic smooth nonlinearities, yet
for the short pulse initial data (
1.11
), the first null condition is not only a sufficient but generally a necessary condition
for the global existence.

Remark 1.7
.

For the case of
n\geq 5
in (
1.1
), when the analogous short pulse initial data (
1.11
) are given
and the constraint condition (
1.12
) is replaced by

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r})^{k}\Omega^{\kappa}\partial^{q}\phi|_{t=1}=O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|})\quad\text{for $0\leq k\leq N_{0}=[\frac{n}{2}]+1$}

(1.19)

with
\Omega\in\{x^{i}\partial_{j}-x^{j}\partial_{i}:1\leq i<j\leq n\}
, then under the first null condition and
suitable scope of
\varepsilon_{0}
, (
1.1
) will have a global smooth solution
\phi
as in Theorem
1.1
.
However, with the increasing of
n
and
N_{0}
, it is more difficult to find
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
such that (
1.19
)
is fulfilled since (
1.19
) is over-determined for the general form of equation (
1.1
).
The choice of
N_{0}=[\frac{n}{2}]+1
comes essentially from the Sobolev imbedding theorem on the sphere
S^{n-1}

(see Lemma 11.1 of Section 11 below).

Remark 1.8
.

In
[
35
]
or
[
41
]
, due to the structure
of the semilinear wave equation systems or the divergence structure of the specific relativistic
membrane equation, suitable
(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1})
can be chosen such that (
1.13
) holds for sufficiently large
N_{0}
.
Note that the largeness of
N_{0}
plays an important role in the analysis of
[
35
]
and
[
41
]
, which leads actually
to solve the global small data solution problem rather than the
large value solution problem in the interior of the light cone. On the other hand, near the light conic surface,
the global smooth solution
\varphi
is obtained through the energy method and by the special
structure of related nonlinear equations (see
[
35
]
and
[
41
]
). However, it seems that the
method in
[
35
,
41
]
fails to the case
for the general form of (
1.10
) here and the restricted number
k
in (
1.12
).

Remark 1.9
.

It should be noted that there have been a few studies on the local or global existence of solutions
to some special semilinear wave equations and hyperbolic systems in multi-dimensions with general short pulse initial
data by matched asymptotic analysis methods, see
[
5
,
6
,
7
,
21
,
34
]
.
For examples, for the semilinear symmetric hyperbolic systems
\partial_{t}u+\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{j}(t,x)\partial_{x_{j}}u=G(t,x,u)

with the smooth pulse initial data

u_{0}(0,x)=u_{0}(x,\frac{\varphi(x)}{\delta})
(
\partial_{x}\varphi(x)\not=0
)
or the semilinear wave equations
\square w+|\partial_{t}w|^{p-1}\partial_{t}w=0
with the smooth and spherically symmetric pulse initial data

(w,\partial_{t}w)(0,x)=(\delta^{\nu+1}w_{0}(r,\frac{r-r_{0}}{\delta}),\delta^{\nu}w_{1}(r,\frac{r-r_{0}}{\delta}))
(
p>1,\nu>0
),
the authors investigated the local existence of
u
in
[
5
,
6
]
and
the local/global existence of
w
in
[
7
]
under various assumptions
of
p
and
\nu
.
In addition, for the quasilinear multidimensional hyperbolic systems, the formal asymptotic correctors have been discussed
in
[
21
]
and
[
34
]
.

Next we give some direct applications for Theorem
1.1
and Remark
1.6
. First, consider
the 4D compressible isentropic Euler equations

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\rho+div(\rho v)=0,\\
&\partial_{t}(\rho v)+div(\rho v\otimes v)+\nabla p=0,\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(1.20)

which express the conservations of the mass and momentum, respectively. Where
(t,x)\in[1,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
\nabla=(\partial_{1},\partial_{2},\partial_{3},\partial_{4})=(\partial_{x^{1}},\partial_{x^{2}},\partial_{x^{3}},\partial_{x^{4}})
,
v=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4})
,
\rho
,
p
stand for the velocity, density, pressure
respectively. For the Chaplygin gases, the equation of state (see
[
12
]
) is

p(\rho)=P_{0}-\frac{B}{\rho}

(1.21)

with
P_{0}
and
B
being positive constants.

Suppose that (
1.20
) admits the irrotational smooth initial data

\big(\rho,v)(1,x)=(\bar{\rho}+\rho_{0}(x),v_{1}^{0}(x),v_{2}^{0}(x),

v_{3}^{0}(x),v_{4}^{0}(x)\big)
,
where
\bar{\rho}+\rho_{0}(x)>0
, and
\bar{\rho}
is
a positive constant which can be normalized so that the sound speed
c(\bar{\rho})=\sqrt{p^{\prime}(\bar{\rho})}=1
. Then by the irrotationality
of (
1.20
), one can
introduce a potential function
\phi
such that
v=\nabla\phi
, and the Bernoulli’s law,
\partial_{t}\phi+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\phi|^{2}+h(\rho)=0
,
holds with the enthalpy
h(\rho)
satisfying
h^{\prime}(\rho)=\frac{c^{2}(\rho)}{\rho}
and
h(\bar{\rho})=0
. Therefore,
for smooth irrotational flows, (
1.20
) is equivalent to that for the Chaplygin gases,

\begin{split}\partial_{t}^{2}\phi-(1+2\partial_{t}\phi)\triangle\phi+2\sum_{k=1}^{4}\partial_{k}\phi\partial_{tk}\phi+\sum_{i,j=1}^{4}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi\partial_{ij}^{2}\phi-|\nabla\phi|^{2}\triangle\phi=0,\end{split}

(1.22)

which
satisfies the first null condition in (
1.16
).
Therefore, for the short pulse initial data (
1.11
) with (
1.12
), Theorem
1.1
applies, that is,

Corollary 1.1
.

For the 4D quasilinear equation (
1.22
) equipped with (
1.11
) and (
1.12
),
when
\delta>0
is small and
0<\varepsilon_{0}\leq\frac{7}{144}
, it has a global smooth solution
\phi
for
t\geq 1
.

In addition, the 4D relativistic membrane equation and 4D nonlinear membrane equation are, respectively,

\partial_{t}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{t}\phi}{\sqrt{1-(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+|\nabla\phi|^{2}}}\Big)-\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{4}\partial_{i}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{i}\phi}{\sqrt{1-(\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}+|\nabla\phi|^{2}}}\Big)=0

(1.23)

and

\partial_{t}^{2}\phi-\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{4}\partial_{i}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{i}\phi}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla\phi|^{2}}}\Big)=0.

(1.24)

Obviously, both (
1.23
) and (
1.24
) fulfill the first null condition (
1.16
) since they don’t
contain the quadratic nonlinearity. Hence, Theorem
1.1
implies

Corollary 1.2
.

For the 4D equations (
1.23
) and (
1.24
) equipped with (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), when
\delta>0
is small and
0<\varepsilon_{0}\leq\frac{7}{144}
, they have global smooth solutions for
t\geq 1
.

1.2.
Sketch for the proof of Theorem
1.1

Since the proof of Theorem
1.1
is rather lengthy, for the convenience of readers, we will outline the main
steps and ideas of the analysis in this subsection. Set
C_{0}=\{(t,x):t\geq 1+2\delta,t=r\}
,
the outermost outgoing conic surface;
A_{2\delta}=\{(t,x):t\geq 1+2\delta,0\leq t-r\leq 2\delta\}
, a domain
containing
C_{0}
; and
B_{2\delta}=\{(t,x):t\geq 1+\delta,t-r\geq 2\delta\}
, a conic domain inside the light cone

\{(t,x):t\geq 1+2\delta,r\leq t\}
with the
lateral boundary given by
\tilde{C}_{2\delta}=\{(t,x):t\geq 1+2\delta,t-r=2\delta\}
. The proof of Theorem
1.1

consists of three parts: the local existence of solution
\phi
for
t\in[1,1+2\delta]
, the global existence of
the solution in
A_{2\delta}
, and the global existence in
B_{2\delta}
.

1.2.1.
Local existence of the solution
\phi
for
t\in[1,1+2\delta]

By the energy method and the first null condition (
1.16
), we can obtain the local existence of smooth solution

\phi
to (
1.10
)-(
1.12
) satisfying
|\partial^{q}\Omega^{l}\phi(t,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}}
for
t\in[1,1+2\delta]
. More importantly, the following basic
estimates for
\phi(1+2\delta,x)
can be derived:

\displaystyle|L^{a}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(1+2\delta,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}}\quad\text{for $r\in[1-2\delta,1+2\delta]$},

(1.25)

\displaystyle|\underline{L}^{a}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(1+2\delta,x)|\lesssim\delta^{3-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}}\quad\text{for $r\in[1-3\delta,1+\delta]$},

(1.26)

where
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
can be chosen as large as needed,

L=\partial_{t}+\partial_{r}
and
\underline{L}=\partial_{t}-\partial_{r}
. Note that (
1.25
) and (
1.26
)
present the better smallness property of
\phi
along certain directional derivatives in different space domains
than that on the whole hypersurface
\{(t,x):t=1+2\delta\}

(i.e.,
|L^{a}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(1+2\delta,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-a-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}}
and
|\underline{L}^{a}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(1+2\delta,x)|\lesssim\delta^{3-a-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}}
).
Based on the crucial estimates (
1.25
)-(
1.26
) together with the first null condition and a series of
involved analyses,
one can estimate
|L^{k}\partial^{q}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}}t^{-3/2-k}
on
\tilde{C}_{2\delta}=\{(t,x):t\geq 1+2\delta,t-r=2\delta\}
eventually, which is one of the key points
for proving the global existence of smooth solution
\phi
in
B_{2\delta}
in the future.

1.2.2.
Global existence of the solution
\phi
in
A_{2\delta}

Motivated strongly by the strategy of D. Christodoulou in
[
9
]
,
we start to construct the solution
\phi
of (
1.10
) in
A_{2\delta}
by taking somewhat more general computations
from the Lorentzian geometric framework which is suitable for treating the 4D general quasilinear wave equation (
1.10
).
Although the main result in
[
9
]
deals with the finite time blowup of smooth solutions
to the 3-D compressible Euler equations, which seems to be different from our goal to establish the global smooth solution to
(
1.10
) with (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), yet
we can still make full use of the idea in
[
9
]
to study whether the outgoing characteristic surfaces collapse
since the intersection of characteristic surfaces leads to the finite time formation of shock waves (also see
[
11
,
36
,
39
]
),
while the expansion of characteristic surfaces generally implies the global existence of smooth solutions to
the hyperbolic systems as indicated in
[
12
,
42
,
43
,
44
]
.
In this process, the key ingredients here are to derive both the precise order of smallness for
\delta

and the suitable time-decay rates for
the solution
\phi
and its derivatives in
A_{2\delta}
.

Following
[
9
]
or
[
39
]
, for a given smooth solution
\phi
to (
1.10
),
one can define an
“optical function”

u
for (
1.10
) as

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{4}g^{\alpha\beta}(\phi,\partial\phi)\partial_{\alpha}{u}\partial_{\beta}{u}=0,\\
&u(1+2\delta,x)=1+2\delta-r\end{aligned}\right.

(1.27)

and subsequently the inverse foliation density

\mu=-\displaystyle(\sum_{\alpha=0}^{4}g^{\alpha 0}\partial_{\alpha}u)^{-1},

(1.28)

where
g^{\alpha 0}=g^{\alpha 0}(\phi,\partial\phi)
. Set the metric
g=\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{4}g_{\alpha\beta}dx^{\alpha}dx^{\beta}

with
(g_{\alpha\beta})
being the inverse matrix of
(g^{\alpha\beta})
and let

\mathring{L}=-\mu\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta=0}^{4}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\partial_{\beta}.

(1.29)

Extend the local coordinates
(\theta^{1},\theta^{2},\theta^{3})
on the standard sphere
\mathbb{S}^{3}
by

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathring{L}\vartheta^{A}=0,\\
&\vartheta^{A}|_{t=1+2\delta}=\theta^{A},\end{aligned}\right.

(1.30)

where and below
A=1,2,3
. As in
[
39
]
or
[
36
]
, one can perform the change of coordinates:

(t,x^{1},x^{2},x^{3},

x^{4})\longrightarrow(s,u,\vartheta^{1},\vartheta^{2},\vartheta^{3})
in
A_{2\delta}
with

\displaystyle s=t,\quad u=u(t,x),\quad\vartheta^{A}=\vartheta^{A}(t,x).

(1.31)

Then
X_{A}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta^{A}}
are the tangent vectors on the curved
sphere
S_{s,u}:=\{(s^{\prime},u^{\prime},\vartheta^{1},\vartheta^{2},\vartheta^{3}):s^{\prime}=s,u^{\prime}=u\}
.

Under the bootstrap assumptions on the suitable smallness and time-decay rates of
(\phi,\partial\phi)
(more details
see
(\star)
in Section
5
) and with the help of the first null condition (
1.16
),

\mu
satisfies
\mathring{L}\mu=O(\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}s^{-5/2}\mu+\delta^{1-2\varepsilon_{0}}s^{-3/2})
.
By
\mu(1+2\delta,x)\sim 1
,
\mathring{L}\sim\partial_{t}+\partial_{r}
and through the integration along integral
curves of
\mathring{L}
,
\mu\sim 1
can
be derived for small
\delta>0
. The positivity of
\mu
indicates that the outgoing characteristic conic surfaces never
intersect as long as the smooth solution
\phi

exists. Set
\varphi=(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3},\varphi_{4}):=(\partial_{t}\phi,\partial_{1}\phi,\partial_{2}\phi,\partial_{3}\phi,\partial_{4}\phi)
.
Then a quasilinear wave system for
(\phi,\varphi)
is derived from (
1.10
)

\begin{split}&\mu\Box_{g}\phi=G(\phi,\varphi,\partial\varphi),\\
&\mu\Box_{g}\varphi_{\gamma}=F_{\gamma}(\phi,\varphi,\partial\varphi),\quad\gamma=0,1,2,3,4,\end{split}

(1.32)

where
\Box_{g}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\det g|}}\partial_{\alpha}(\sqrt{|\det g|}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta})
,
G
and
F_{\gamma}
are smooth functions
of their arguments.
To study the nonlinear system (
1.32
), as usual, we first carry out the energy estimate
for its linearized equation

\mu\Box_{g}\Psi=\Phi.

(1.33)

If
\Psi=\Psi_{k}=Z^{k}\varphi
is chosen in (
1.33
),
where
Z
is one of some suitable first order vector fields, then it follows from
(
1.32
) and direct computations of the commutator
[\mu\Box_{g},Z^{k}]

that the term
\Phi=\Phi_{k}
in (
1.33
) will contain the
(k+2)
-th order derivatives of
\varphi
.
Especially,
\not{\nabla}Z^{k-1}\chi
and
\not{\nabla}^{2}Z^{k-1}\mu
appear in
\Phi_{k}
,
where
\not{\nabla}
represents the covariant derivative on
S_{s,u}
,
and
\chi
stands for the second fundamental form of
S_{s,u}
which is defined as

\chi_{AB}=g(\mathscr{D}_{X_{A}}\mathring{L},X_{B})

with
\mathscr{D}
being the Levi-Civita connection of
g
.
Utilizing the energy estimate of
\Psi_{k}
derived from (
1.33
),
one then can bound the
L^{2}
norms of
(k+1)
order derivatives of
\varphi
by
L^{2}
norm of

\Phi_{k}
and further by the
L^{2}
norms of
(k+2)
order derivatives of
\varphi
.
However, this obviously leads to the loss of derivatives for
\varphi
, and hence,
the estimate of
(k+1)
order derivatives of
\varphi

can not be obtained directly from the energy estimate for the equation
\mu\Box_{g}\Psi_{k}=\Phi_{k}
.
To overcome the difficulty, motivated by some ideas in
[
9
]
, one can use the following strategies:

{\bf\bullet}
First, one can derive the transport equation for
\textrm{tr}\chi
along the vector field
\mathring{L}
,
where
\textrm{tr}\chi
is the trace of
\chi
on
S_{s,u}
. Note that due to the dependence of
g^{\alpha\beta}
on
\phi
and
\varphi
,
it is required to express
\mathring{L}\textrm{tr}\chi
by making use of the equation of
\phi
in (
1.32
), which leads to the
requirement on the estimate of
\phi
. Second, by
utilizing the Ricci identity, one can find the relation between
\not{\nabla}\chi
and
\not{\nabla}\textrm{tr}\chi

from (
1.32
). By these properties and some elaborate analysis,
the
L^{2}
estimates for the highest order derivatives of
\not{\nabla}\chi
are obtained by taking
L^{2}

integration along integral curves of
\mathring{L}
. In this process, we have to
establish some new estimates for the Riemann curvature on
S_{s,u}
of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, which is achieved with the aid of the Christoffel symbols
on
S_{s,u}
(see Lemma
9.1
).

{\bf\bullet}
According to the elliptic estimate on
\not{\nabla}^{2}f
from
\not{\triangle}f
(see details in (
9.329
)),
where
\not{\triangle}
is the Laplacian operator on
S_{s,u}
, the
L^{2}
norm of the highest order derivative
of
\not{\nabla}\mu
can be controlled by the quantities containing the derivatives of
\not{\triangle}\mu
and

\not{\nabla}\mu
. Thanks to the transport equation for
\not{\triangle}\mu
, the
estimates on the derivatives of
\not{\triangle}\mu
and

\not{\nabla}\mu
may be derived as for
\not{d}{\textrm{tr}}\chi
.

{\bf\bullet}
Based on the above estimates for the highest order derivatives of
\chi
and
\mu
, all the terms in
\Phi_{k}

can be treated by careful classifications. Together with the derived energy estimates on the solution
\Psi
of (
1.33
)
and some Sobolev-type
inequalities on the sphere, both the better time-decay rate
s^{-5/2}
in the right hand side of the
equation
\mathring{L}\varphi=O(\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}s^{-5/2})
and the better
L^{\infty}
bound of
\varphi
with order
\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}s^{-3/2}

are obtained. Then the basic bootstrap assumptions of
(\phi,\varphi)
can be closed.

On the other hand, we also need to pay close attention to the treatments of the solution
\phi
itself and
L\phi

since the coefficients in (
1.10
) includes
\phi
while it is extremely difficult to derive an effective governing equation for
L\phi
.
More importantly, it should be noted that both the
precise time-decay rate and smallness of
L\phi
on
\tilde{C}_{2\delta}
, such as
|L^{k}\phi(t,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}t^{-3/2-k}

for
(t,x)\in\tilde{C}_{2\delta}
, will be crucial in order to show the global solvability of (
1.10
) in

B_{2\delta}
since the related boundary integrals on
\tilde{C}_{2\delta}
in the energy estimates require the
suitable smallness. We will use the following procedure to derive the desired time-decay rates and smallness of
(\phi,L\phi)
:

{\bf\bullet}
We first carry out the energy estimate on
\varphi_{\gamma}
by the equation
\mu\Box_{g}\varphi_{\gamma}=F_{\gamma}(\phi,\varphi,\partial\varphi)
. Then to avoid reconstructing the analogous and complex energy inequalities
for
\phi
from the equation
\mu\Box_{g}\phi=G(\phi,\varphi,\partial\varphi)
, we utilize the direct relationship

\varphi_{\gamma}=\partial_{x^{\gamma}}\phi
to get the
L^{2}
norm of
\phi
by the energies of
\varphi_{\gamma}
.
Based on this and Sobolev’s embedding theorem, the
L^{\infty}
estimates of
\phi
and its derivatives can be obtained.

{\bf\bullet}
Note that all the estimates of
\phi
in
A_{2\delta}
are made in the modified frame
\{\mathring{L},T,R_{ij}\}
,
where
\mathring{L}
,
T
,
R_{ij}
are some suitable vectors approximating
L
,
-\partial_{r}=\frac{\underline{L}-L}{2}
,

\Omega_{ij}=x^{i}\partial_{j}-x^{j}\partial_{i}
respectively on the time
t=1+2\delta
. To get the
L^{\infty}
estimates of
Z^{\alpha}\phi

with
Z\in\{L,\underline{L},\Omega_{ij}\}

and further obtain the required time-decay rate of
L\phi
, it is required to express the vector fields
\{L,\underline{L},\Omega_{ij}\}

in terms of
\{\mathring{L},T,R_{ij}\}
. A typical such expression is
L=c_{L}\mathring{L}+c_{T}T+c_{\Omega}^{ij}\Omega_{ij}
,
where
c_{L}
,
c_{T}
and
c^{ij}_{\Omega}
are
smooth functions. One expects that
L\phi
and its derivatives can admit the desired smallness of higher orders with respect to
\delta

and faster time-decay rate. However,
T\phi
and its derivatives have not good enough properties as needed,
it is hoped that the coefficient
c_{T}
can make up for these deficiencies. Although the general form of (
1.10
)
makes it difficult to analyze the time-decay rates of the related coefficients
c_{T}
,
c^{ij}_{\Omega}
and so on,
thanks to the first null condition (
1.16
), one can find their transport equations along the directional derivative
\mathring{L}
.
Based on this, the demanded estimates of
c_{L}
,
c_{T}
and
c^{ij}_{\Omega}
, then the estimate of
(\phi,L\phi)

can be obtained.

When the desired time-decay rates and the smallness estimates of
(\phi,\varphi)
are established, the
global existence of the solution to (
1.10
) with (
1.11
)-(
1.12
)
in
A_{2\delta}
is shown.

1.2.3.
Global existence of solution
\phi
inside
B_{2\delta}

It follows from the local estimates of the solution
\phi
to (
1.10
) with (
1.11
)-(
1.12
) in
Theorem
3.1
of Section 3 that for
r\in[1-3\delta,1+\delta]
,

\partial^{q}\phi(1+2\delta,x)
admit the high order smallness
O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}})
(compared with
\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}}
).
In addition, it has been known that the outgoing characteristic conic surfaces starting from the
domain
\{t=1+2\delta,1-4\delta\leq r\leq 1+\delta\}
in
A_{4\delta}

are almost straight, and then the resulting domain contains the boundary
\tilde{C}_{2\delta}

of
B_{2\delta}
. Noting (
1.25
)-(
1.26
) and by the estimates in
A_{4\delta}
we have established
that on
\tilde{C}_{2\delta}
the solution
\phi
and its derivatives
satisfy
|L^{k}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}t^{-3/2-k}
,
whose right hand side admits the higher order smallness
O(\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}})
(see (
11.419
) of Section 11).
Based on such “good” smallness and time-decay rate
t^{-3/2}
of
\phi
on
\tilde{C}_{2\delta}
,
one can solve the global Goursat problem of (
1.10
) in
B_{2\delta}
.
To this end, we will carry out the global weighted energy estimates for the solution
\phi

in
B_{2\delta}
and make use of the Klainerman-Sobolev inequality to get the time-decay rates of
\partial^{\alpha}\phi
.
However, since the classical Klainerman-Sobolev inequality holds generally on the whole space (see Proposition 6.5.1 in
[
20
]
),
we need a modified and refined Klainerman-Sobolev inequality in
B_{2\delta}
.
Using this together with the bootstrap energy assumptions,
the global Goursat problem of (
1.10
) in
B_{2\delta}
can be solved by direct energy
estimates although this is not the usual small data solution problem.

1.3.
Organization of the paper

Our paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, the short pulse initial data (
1.11
)
satisfying (
1.12
) are shown to exist under the null condition (
1.16
). In Section
3
, the local
existence of the solution
\phi

for
1\leq t\leq 1+2\delta
is established by the energy method. In Section
4
, we give some preliminaries on
the related differential geometry.
In addition, the equation of
\mu
is derived, and some basic calculations
are given for the covariant derivatives
of the null frame and the deformation tensors. The crucial bootstrap assumptions

(\star)
in
A_{2\delta}
are given in the beginning of Section
5
, meanwhile,
under assumptions
(\star)
we obtain some estimates of several important quantities which
will be extensively used in subsequent sections. In Section
6
, under the assumptions
(\star)
,
the higher order smallness for the
L^{\infty}
estimates of
(\phi,\varphi)
in
A_{2\delta}
are established.
In Section
7
, we carry out the energy estimates for the linearized equation
\mu\Box_{g}\Psi=\Phi

and define some suitable higher order weighted energies and fluxes as in
[
39
]
. In Section
8
,
under the assumptions
(\star)
,
the
L^{2}
estimates for the higher order derivatives of several basic quantities are derived by

\sqrt{E_{1,\leq k+2}(t,u)}
and

\sqrt{E_{2,\leq k+2}(t,u)}
,
where the energies
E_{1,\leq k+2}(t,u)
and
E_{2,\leq k+2}(t,u)
contain the highest order derivatives of

\varphi
. In Section
9
, the

L^{2}
estimates for the highest order derivatives of tr
\chi
and
\not{\triangle}\mu
are established. In Section
10
,
we deal with the error terms appeared in the energy inequality of Section
7
.
Based on all the estimates in Section
5
-
10
, in Section
11
we complete the bootstrap argument by choosing the
assumptions
(\star)
and further establish the global existence of solution
\phi
to equation (
1.10
)
in
A_{2\delta}
. In addition, in the end of Section
11
,
the delicate estimates of
\phi
on
\tilde{C}_{2\delta}
are derived. Finally,
we prove the global existence of solution
\phi
inside
B_{2\delta}
and complete the proof of Theorem
1.1

in Section
12
.

1.4.
Notations

Through the whole paper, unless stated otherwise, Greek indices
\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\cdots\}
correspond to the
time-space coordinates in
\{0,1,2,3,4\}
, Latin indices
\{i,j,a,b,\cdots\}
correspond to the
spatial coordinates in
\{1,2,3,4\}
, and the Einstein summation convention is used to sum over repeated upper
and lower indices. In addition,
f_{1}\lesssim f_{2}
means that there exists a generic positive constant

C
independent of the parameter
\delta>0
in (
1.11
) and the variables
(t,x)
such that
f_{1}\leq Cf_{2}
.

Also, the coefficients
g^{\alpha\beta}(\phi,\partial\phi)
are denoted as
g^{\alpha\beta}
for convenience,
and for the Lorentz metric
(g_{\alpha\beta})
,
(g^{\alpha\beta})
represents its inverse spacetime metric.
Finally, the following notations will be used:

\displaystyle\omega^{i}:=\omega_{i}=\frac{x^{i}}{r},\quad i=1,2,3,4,

\displaystyle\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3},\omega_{4}),

\displaystyle t_{0}:=1+2\delta,

\displaystyle\partial_{x}:=(\partial_{1},\partial_{2},\partial_{3},\partial_{4}),

\displaystyle L:=\partial_{t}+\partial_{r},

\displaystyle\underline{L}:=\partial_{t}-\partial_{r},

\displaystyle\Omega_{ij}:=x^{i}\partial_{j}-x^{j}\partial_{i},

\displaystyle S:=t\partial_{t}+r\partial_{r}=\frac{t-r}{2}\underline{L}+\frac{t+r}{2}L,

\displaystyle H_{i}:=t\partial_{i}+x^{i}\partial_{t}=\omega_{i}\big(\frac{r-t}{2}\underline{L}+\frac{t+r}{2}L\big)-\frac{t}{r}\omega^{j}\Omega_{ij},

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t}:=\{(s,x):s=t,x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\}.

2.
On the short pulse initial data

In this section, under the first null condition (
1.16
), we show the existence of the short pulse initial data
(
1.11
) with property (
1.12
).
Set
\tilde{\chi}(s)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with

\tilde{\chi}(s)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &0\quad\text{if $s\leq 0$},\\
&1\quad\text{if $s\geq 1$},\end{aligned}\right.

and the re-scaled function
\chi_{\delta}(t,x)=\tilde{\chi}(\frac{|x|-t+\delta}{\delta})
.

Assume that
\psi(t,x)
satisfies

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\bar{g}^{\alpha\beta}(\psi,\partial\psi)\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\psi=0,\\
&\psi(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta,x)=\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}/3}\psi_{0}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega),\partial_{0}\psi(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta,x)=\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}/3}\psi_{1}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega),\end{aligned}\right.

(2.34)

where

\bar{g}^{\alpha\beta}(\psi,\partial\psi)=m^{\alpha\beta}+\chi_{\delta}\big(g^{\alpha\beta,0}\psi+\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\psi\big)+\chi_{\delta}^{2}h^{\alpha\beta}(\psi,\partial\psi),

\psi_{0}(s,\omega)
and
\psi_{1}(s,\omega)
are smooth functions with compact supports being in
\{(s,\omega):-1<s<-\frac{1}{2},\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{3}\}
,

\varsigma>0
is a suitable constant which will be determined later, with
m^{\alpha\beta},g^{\alpha\beta,0},\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}
and
h^{\alpha\beta}
given in (
1.2
).
We next prove that (
2.34
) has a smooth solution for
t\in[1-\frac{1}{2}\delta,1]
.

Suppose that for
1-\frac{1}{2}\delta\leq t\leq 1

and
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
N_{0}\geq 6
,

|\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\psi(t,x)|\leq\delta^{3/2-|q|}\quad(|q|+|\kappa|\leq N_{0}).

(2.35)

Define the following energy for (
2.34
) and for
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

M_{n}(t):=\sum_{|q|+|\kappa|\leq n}\delta^{2|q|}\|\partial\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\psi(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{2}.

Set
w=\delta^{|q|}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\psi
with
|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2N_{0}-2
. Then it follows from (
2.34
)
and direct computations that

\displaystyle\int_{1-\frac{1}{2}\delta}^{t}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}(\bar{g}^{\beta\gamma}\partial_{\beta\gamma}^{2}w\partial_{0}w)(\tau,x)dxd\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{t}}\big(\bar{g}^{0\beta}\partial_{\beta}w\partial_{0}w-\frac{1}{2}\bar{g}^{\beta\gamma}\partial_{\beta}w\partial_{\gamma}w\big)dx-\int_{\Sigma_{1-\frac{1}{2}\delta}}\big(\bar{g}^{0\beta}\partial_{\beta}w\partial_{0}w-\frac{1}{2}\bar{g}^{\beta\gamma}\partial_{\beta}w\partial_{\gamma}w\big)dx

\displaystyle-\int_{1-\frac{1}{2}\delta}^{t}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\big((\partial_{\gamma}\bar{g}^{\gamma\beta})\partial_{\beta}w\partial_{0}w-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\tau}\bar{g}^{\beta\gamma})\partial_{\beta}w\partial_{\gamma}w\big)(\tau,x)dxd\tau

(2.36)

with

\begin{split}\bar{g}^{\beta\gamma}\partial_{\beta\gamma}^{2}w=&\delta^{|q|}[\bar{g}^{\beta\gamma}\partial_{\beta\gamma}^{2},\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}]\psi\\
=&\delta^{|q|}\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle|\kappa_{1}|+|\kappa_{2}|\leq|\kappa|,\\
|q_{1}|+|q_{2}|\leq|q|+2,|q_{2}|\geq 2,\\
|q_{1}|+|\kappa_{1}|\geq 1\end{array}}(\partial^{q_{1}}\Omega^{\kappa_{1}}\bar{g}^{\beta\gamma})(\partial^{q_{2}}\Omega^{\kappa_{2}}\psi),\end{split}

(2.37)

where the unnecessary constant coefficients in the expression of (
2.37
) are neglected.

It follows from the bootstrap assumption (
2.35
) and (
2
) that

\begin{split}&\int_{\Sigma_{t}}\big((\partial_{t}w)^{2}+|\partial_{x}w|^{2}\big)(t,x)dx\\
\lesssim&\int_{\Sigma_{1-\frac{1}{2}\delta}}\big((\partial_{t}w)^{2}+|\partial_{x}w|^{2}\big)(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta,x)dx+\int_{1-\frac{1}{2}\delta}^{t}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\delta^{-1/2}\big((\partial_{t}w)^{2}+|\partial_{x}w|^{2}\big)(\tau,x)dxd\tau\\
&+\int_{1-\frac{1}{2}\delta}^{t}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}|\bar{g}^{\beta\gamma}\partial_{\beta\gamma}^{2}w\partial_{t}w|(\tau,x)dxd\tau.\end{split}

(2.38)

Utilizing (
2.35
) and (
2.37
) yields

\begin{split}|\bar{g}^{\beta\gamma}\partial_{\beta\gamma}^{2}w|\lesssim&\sum_{\tiny\begin{array}[]{c}2\leq|q_{2}|\leq|q|+2,\ |\kappa_{2}|\leq|\kappa|,\\
|q_{2}|+|\kappa_{2}|\leq|q|+|\kappa|+1\end{array}}\delta^{|q_{2}|-\frac{3}{2}}|\partial^{q_{2}}\Omega^{\kappa_{2}}\psi|\\
+&\sum_{|q_{1}|\leq|q|,\ |\kappa_{1}|\leq|\kappa|}\delta^{|q_{1}|-\frac{1}{2}}\big(|\partial^{q_{1}}\Omega^{\kappa_{1}}\psi|+|\partial\partial^{q_{1}}\Omega^{\kappa_{1}}\psi|\big).\end{split}

(2.39)

Due to
\tau\in[1-\frac{1}{2}\delta,1]
, it then holds that

\|\partial^{q_{1}}\Omega^{\kappa_{1}}\psi(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}\lesssim\delta\|\partial\partial^{q_{1}}\Omega^{\kappa_{1}}\psi(\tau,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}.

(2.40)

Substituting (
2.39
) and (
2.40
) into (
2.38
), one gets from the Gronwall’s inequality that for
1-\frac{1}{2}\delta\leq t\leq 1
,

\begin{split}M_{2N_{0}-2}(t)\lesssim M_{2N_{0}-2}(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta)\lesssim\delta^{3-2\varepsilon_{0}/3}.\end{split}

(2.41)

We next close the bootstrap assumption (
2.35
). By the Sobolev’s imbedding theorem on the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{3}_{r}

with center at the origin and radius
r
, one has

|w(t,x)|\lesssim\frac{1}{r^{3/2}}\|\Omega^{\leq 2}w\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{3}_{r})}.

This, together with
r\sim 1
for
t\in[1-\frac{1}{2}\delta,1]
,
(t,x)\in\textrm{supp}\ w

and (
2.41
), yields

|\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\psi(t,x)|\lesssim\|\Omega^{\leq 2}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{3}_{r})}\lesssim\delta^{1/2}\|\partial\Omega^{\leq 2}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}/3},\quad

(2.42)

where
|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2N_{0}-4
. Thus, for small
\delta>0
and
0<\varepsilon_{0}<1
, (
2.35
) is proved.

Next we improve the
L^{\infty}
estimate of
\psi(1,x)
on some special space domains. To this end,
rewrite the equation in (
2.34
) as

\begin{split}\underline{L}L\psi=&\frac{3}{2r}L\psi-\frac{3}{2r}\underline{L}\psi+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sum_{i}\omega^{j}\omega^{a}\Omega_{ij}\Omega_{ia}\psi\\
&+{\chi}_{\delta}\big(g^{\alpha\beta,0}\psi+\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\psi\big)\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\psi+{\chi_{\delta}}^{2}h^{\alpha\beta}(\psi,\partial\psi)\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\psi\end{split}

(2.43)

with
|\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\psi\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\psi|\lesssim(|L\psi|+|\Omega\psi|)|\partial^{2}\psi|+|\partial\psi|(|L\partial\psi|+|\Omega\partial\psi|)

due to the first null condition (
1.16
).
It follows from (
2.42
) that the worst term in the expression of
\underline{L}L\psi

is
{\chi_{\delta}}\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\psi\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\psi
. Then this yields
|\underline{L}L\psi|\lesssim\delta^{1-2\varepsilon_{0}/3}
.
Together with
the vanishing property of
\psi
on
\{t=r\}
,
integrating
\underline{L}L\psi
along integral curves of
\underline{L}
gives that for
(t,r)\in D

(see Figure 2 below),

|L\psi(t,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-2\varepsilon_{0}/3}.

(2.44)

Analogously, one has that for
(t,r)\in D
,

|L\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\psi(t,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-2\varepsilon_{0}/3}\quad\text{for $|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2N_{0}-6$}.

(2.45)

Substituting (
2.45
) into the expression of
\underline{L}L\psi
again, and noting that the first null condition (
1.16
)
gives

|\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\psi\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\psi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}
,
then one can see that the worst term
in the expression of
\underline{L}L\psi
becomes
-\frac{3}{2r}\underline{L}\psi
.
This shows
|\underline{L}L\psi|\lesssim\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}/3}

for
(t,r)\in D
by (
2.42
) and (
2.45
). Hence the following improved estimate is obtained

|L\psi(t,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}/3},\quad(t,r)\in D.

Similar arguments lead to

|L\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\psi(1,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}/3}\quad\text{for $|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2N_{0}-7$
and $1-\delta\leq r\leq 1$}.

Analogously, for
r\in[1-\delta,1]
, the induction argument yields

|L^{k}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\psi(1,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}/3},\quad 3k+|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2N_{0}-4.

(2.46)

Figure 2. The domain
D

Choose the initial data for (
1.10
) as

\left\{\begin{aligned} \phi(1,x)&=(\chi_{\delta}\psi)(1,x),\\
\partial_{t}\phi(1,x)&=(\partial_{t}(\chi_{\delta}\psi))(1,x).\end{aligned}\right.

(2.47)

Obviously, the support of
\phi(1,x)
lies in
\{x:1-\delta<r<1\}
(see Figure 3 below).

Figure 3. Support of
\phi(1,x)

Next we check that the initial data (
2.47
) satisfy (
1.12
).
In fact, it follows (
1.10
) and (
2.34
) that

\left\{\begin{aligned} g^{\alpha\beta}(\phi,\partial\phi)\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}(\phi-\chi_{\delta}\psi)=&{\chi_{\delta}}^{2}(g^{\alpha\beta,0}\psi+\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\psi)\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\psi+{\chi_{\delta}}^{3}h^{\alpha\beta}(\psi,\partial\psi)\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\psi\\
&-(g^{\alpha\beta,0}\phi+\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\phi)\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}(\chi_{\delta}\psi)-h^{\alpha\beta}(\psi,\partial\psi)\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}(\chi_{\delta}\psi)\\
&-\delta^{-1}\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}(\frac{|x|-t+\delta}{\delta})\big(\frac{3}{r}\psi+2L\psi\big),\\
(\phi-\chi_{\delta}\psi)(1,x)=&0,\\
\big(\partial_{t}(\phi-\chi_{\delta}\psi)\big)(1,x)=&0.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.48)

It is obvious that

\displaystyle\big(L(\phi-\chi_{\delta}\psi)\big)(1,x)=0,

(2.49)

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{r}^{2}(\phi-\chi_{\delta}\psi)\big)(1,x)=0,

(2.50)

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t}\partial_{r}(\phi-\chi_{\delta}\psi)\big)(1,x)=0.

(2.51)

In addition, it follows from (
2.43
) and (
2.46
) that

|\underline{L}(\frac{3}{r}\psi+2L\psi)|_{D}\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}
,
which implies

|\frac{3}{r}\psi+2L\psi|_{D}\lesssim\delta^{3-\varepsilon_{0}}.

This, together with (
2.48
) and (
2.46
), deduces

|\partial_{t}^{2}(\phi-\chi_{\delta}\psi)(1,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}.

(2.52)

Therefore, by (
2.50
), (
2.51
) and (
2.52
), one has

|L^{2}(\phi-\chi_{\delta}\psi)(1,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}.

(2.53)

Combining (
2.46
), (
2.48
), (
2.49
) and (
2.53
) yields

\displaystyle|\phi(1,x)|+|L\phi(1,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}/3},\ |L^{2}\phi(1,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}}.

Note that
L^{k}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi

(0\leq k\leq 2)
can be estimated similarly as above after taking suitable derivatives on (
2.48
)
to get the upper bounds of
|L^{k}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}(\phi-\chi_{\delta}\psi)(1,x)|

(0\leq k\leq 2)
and applying (
2.46
) directly. Finally,
we arrive at

\displaystyle|\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(1,x)|+|L\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(1,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}/3},

\displaystyle|L^{2}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(1,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}}.

This means that the initial data (
2.47
) satisfy (
1.12
).

Remark 2.1
.

Rewriting the initial data (
2.47
) as

\left\{\begin{aligned} \phi(1,x)&=\psi(1,\frac{r-1}{\delta}\cdot\delta\omega+\omega)\tilde{\chi}(\frac{r-1}{\delta}+1):=\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}/3}\phi_{0}^{\delta}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega),\\
\partial_{t}\phi(1,x)&=\partial_{t}\psi(1,\frac{r-1}{\delta}\cdot\delta\omega+\omega)\tilde{\chi}(\frac{r-1}{\delta}+1)-\delta^{-1}\psi(1,\frac{r-1}{\delta}\cdot\delta\omega+\omega)\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}(\frac{r-1}{\delta}+1)\\
&:=\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}/3}\phi_{1}^{\delta}(\frac{r-1}{\delta},\omega).\end{aligned}\right.

(2.54)

Then
(\phi_{0}^{\delta},\phi_{1}^{\delta})(s,\omega)
fulfill the property (
1.12
). For brevity and without confusions, we
drop the notation
\delta
in
(\phi_{0}^{\delta},\phi_{1}^{\delta})
as
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
.

3.
Local existence

In this section, we apply the energy method and the distinctive structure of the initial data (
1.11
)
to establish the local existence of the smooth solution
\phi
to
equation (
1.10
) for
1\leq t\leq t_{0}
, meanwhile, several
key estimates of
\phi(t_{0},x)
in some special space domains are derived.

Theorem 3.1
.

Under the assumption (
1.12
) on
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
, when
\delta>0
is small, equation (
1.10
) with (
1.11
)
admits a local smooth solution
\phi\in C^{\infty}([1,t_{0}]\times\mathbb{R}^{4})
. Moreover, for
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
q\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{5}
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{6}
, it holds that

(i)

\displaystyle|L^{m}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(t_{0},x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad r\in[1-2\delta,1+2\delta],

(3.55)

\displaystyle|\underline{L}^{l}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(t_{0},x)|\lesssim\delta^{3-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad r\in[1-3\delta,1+\delta].

(3.56)

(ii)

|\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(t_{0},x)|\lesssim\left\{\begin{aligned} \delta^{3-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad&\text{for}\quad|q|\leq 1,\\
\delta^{4-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad&\text{for}\quad|q|>1,\end{aligned}\right.\quad r\in[1-3\delta,1+\delta].

(3.57)

(iii)

|\underline{L}^{l}L^{m}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(t_{0},x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad r\in[1-2\delta,1+\delta].

(3.58)

Proof.

Denote by
Z_{g}
any fixed vector filed in
\{S,H_{i},i=1,2,3,4\}
, and construct the energies

\tilde{M}_{n}(t):=\sum_{|q|+|\kappa|+k\leq n}\delta^{2|q|}\|\partial\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}Z_{g}^{k}\phi(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})}^{2}

for
n\leq\tilde{N}_{0}
,
k\leq 2
,
\tilde{N}_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\tilde{N}_{0}\geq 4
.

Figure 4. Space-time domain
D^{1}=\{(t,r):1\leq t\leq t_{0},2-t\leq r\leq t\}

For suitably small
\delta>0
, by the same arguments as in the proofs of the local existence of the solution
\psi
to (
2.34
) and the inequality (
2.42
) in
Section
2
(
\bar{g}
and
\psi
are replaced by
g
and
Z_{g}^{k}\phi
respectively in (
2
)-(
2.42
), menawhile
the time interval

[1-\frac{1}{2}\delta,1]
is replace by
[1,1+2\delta]
from (
2.35
) to (
2.42
)),
when
t\in[1,t_{0}]
and by (
1.12
), one can prove that (
1.10
) has a smooth solution
\phi
satisfying

|L^{k}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(t,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}},

(3.59)

where
|q|+|\kappa|+k\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-4
and
k\leq 2
.
Moreover, since
|\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(t,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|}
holds in
D^{1}
when
|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-4
,
after repeating the procedures from (
2.43
)-(
2.46
), we obtain that for
r\in[1-2\delta,1+2\delta]
,

|L^{m}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(t_{0},x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-|q|-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad 3m+|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-4.

(3.60)

Figure 5. Space domain
D^{2}=\{t,r):1\leq t\leq t_{0},2-\delta-t\leq r\leq t-\delta\}

Let
\bar{\partial}\in\{\partial_{t},\partial_{r}\}
. For any indices
q
and
\kappa
with
|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-6
, by virtue of

L\bar{\partial}^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\underline{L}\phi=\bar{\partial}^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}(L\underline{L}\phi),

(3.61)

L\underline{L}\phi=\frac{3}{2r}L\phi-\frac{3}{2r}\underline{L}\phi+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sum_{i}\omega^{j}\omega^{k}\Omega_{ij}\Omega_{ik}\phi+\Big(g^{\alpha\beta,0}\phi+\tilde{g}^{\alpha\beta,\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\phi+h^{\alpha\beta}(\phi,\partial\phi)\Big)\partial_{\alpha\beta}^{2}\phi

(3.62)

and (
3.59
), one has
|L\bar{\partial}^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\underline{L}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|}
.
This yields
|\underline{L}\bar{\partial}^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|}
in
D^{2}

by the integration along integral curves of
L
when
|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-6
(see Figure 5). Thus,
it follows from (
3.61
), (
3.62
) and (
3.59
) that in
D^{2}
,

\begin{split}&|\underline{L}\bar{\partial}^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|},\quad|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-6,\\
&|L\underline{L}\bar{\partial}^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|},\quad|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-7.\end{split}

By an induction argument, it holds that in
D^{2}
,

\begin{split}&|\underline{L}^{l}\bar{\partial}^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|},\quad 2l+|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-4,\\
&|L\underline{L}^{l}\bar{\partial}^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|},\quad 2l+|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-5.\end{split}

(3.63)

This, together with
\partial_{t}=\frac{1}{2}(L+\underline{L})
,
\partial_{i}=\frac{\omega^{i}}{2}(L-\underline{L})-\frac{1}{r}\omega^{j}\Omega_{ij}
, (
3.62
) and (
3.59
), yields that for
2|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-4

and
(t,x)\in D^{2}
,

|\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(t,x)|\lesssim\left\{\begin{aligned} \delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}},\quad&\text{for}\quad|q|\leq 2,\\
\delta^{4-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|},\quad&\text{for}\quad|q|>2.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.64)

Next, we use (
3.62
) to improve the estimates in (
3.63
) and (
3.64
). In fact, acting (
3.62
)
by
\underline{L}^{l-1}\bar{\partial}^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}

(l\geq 1)
, it follows from (
3.63
) and
\partial_{i}={\omega^{i}}\partial_{r}-\frac{1}{r}\omega^{j}\Omega_{ij}

that when
2l+|q|+|\kappa|\lesssim 2\tilde{N}_{0}-5
,

|L\underline{L}^{l}\bar{\partial}^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(t,x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|},\ (t,x)\in D^{2}.

(3.65)

Then integrating (
3.65
) along integral curves of
L
in
D^{2}
yields

|\underline{L}^{l}\bar{\partial}^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(t,x)|\lesssim\delta^{3-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|},\ (t,x)\in D^{2},\ l\geq 1\ \text{and}\ 2l+|q|+|\kappa|\lesssim 2\tilde{N}_{0}-5.

Analogous treatments from (
3.59
) deduce that in
D^{2}
,

\begin{split}&|\bar{\partial}^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(t,x)|\lesssim\delta^{3-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|},\ |q|+|\kappa|\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-4,\\
&|L\bar{\partial}^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(t,x)|\lesssim\delta^{3-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|},\ |q|+|\kappa|\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-5.\end{split}

Therefore, (
3.64
) can be improved into the more refined estimate (
3.57
).
It remains to show (
3.58
). Note that on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
, when
r\in[1-2\delta,1+\delta]

and
2l+|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-5
,
|L\underline{L}^{l}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|}
holds by (
3.63
).
Assume inductively that the following estimate holds for
r\in[1-2\delta,1+\delta]
,

|L^{m}\underline{L}^{l}\partial^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi(t_{0},x)|\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|},\quad\text{where}\left\{\begin{aligned} 1\leq m\leq m_{0},\quad l\geq 1,\\
3m-3+|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2N_{0}-4,\\
m+2l+|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2N_{0}-4.\end{aligned}\right.

(3.66)

We next show that (
3.66
) remains true for
m=m_{0}+1
.
When
3m_{0}-1+|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-4
and
m_{0}+1+2l+|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-4
,
it follows from (
3.66
), (
3.62
) and (
3.60
) that

\begin{split}|L^{m_{0}+1}\underline{L}^{l}\bar{\partial}^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi|&\lesssim|L^{m_{0}+1}\underline{L}^{\leq l-1}\bar{\partial}^{\leq|q|}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi|+\delta^{2-2\varepsilon_{0}-|q|}\\
&\lesssim\cdots\lesssim|L^{m_{0}+1}\bar{\partial}^{\leq|q|}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi|+\delta^{2-2\varepsilon_{0}-|q|}\\
&\lesssim\delta^{2-2\varepsilon_{0}-|q|}.\end{split}

(3.67)

When
m_{0}+1+2l+|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-4
and
3m_{0}+|q|+|\kappa|\leq 2\tilde{N}_{0}-4
,
by (
3.67
) and (
3.62
), one has

|L^{m_{0}+1}\underline{L}^{l}\bar{\partial}^{q}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi|\lesssim|L^{m_{0}+1}\underline{L}^{\leq l-1}\bar{\partial}^{\leq|q|}\Omega^{\kappa}\phi|+\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|}\lesssim\delta^{2-\varepsilon_{0}-|q|}.

(3.68)

Combining (
3.67
) and (
3.68
) yields (
3.66
). Then (
3.58
)
is obtained after setting
|q|=0
in (
3.66
).

Therefore, the proof of Theorem
3.1
is finished.
∎

4.
Some preliminaries

4.1.
The related geometry and definitions

In this subsection, we give some preliminaries on the
related geometry and definitions, which will be
utilized as the basic tools to establish the global existence of the
smooth solution
\phi
to (
1.10
) in
A_{2\delta}
.

The definitions of the optical function
u
and the inverse foliation density
\mu
have been
given in (
1.27
) and (
1.28
) respectively.
Note that

\tilde{L}=-g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\partial_{\beta}

is a tangent vector field of the outgoing light cone
\{u=C\}
. In addition,
\tilde{L}
is geodesic and
\tilde{L}t=\mu^{-1}
.
Thus, one can rescale
\tilde{L}
as

\mathring{L}=\mu\tilde{L},

which approximates
L=\partial_{t}+\partial_{r}
(this is obvious for
t=t_{0}
).

Set
\tilde{T}=-g^{\nu 0}\partial_{\nu}-\mathring{L}
, which is close to
-\partial_{r}
for
t=t_{0}
. Then
by the definition of the null frame, let

\displaystyle T=\mu\tilde{T},\quad\mathring{\underline{L}}=\mu\mathring{L}+2T,

(4.69)

where
\mathring{L}
and
\mathring{\underline{L}}
are two vector fields in the null frame,
moreover
\mathring{\underline{L}}
approximates
\underline{L}=\partial_{t}-\partial_{r}
.

With respect to the other vector fields

\{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}\}
in the null frame, they have been chosen in (
1.30
) with
X_{A}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta^{A}}

(A=1,2,3)
.
Rewrite
X_{A}=X_{A}^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}
. Then
X_{A}^{0}=0
due to
\frac{\partial t}{\partial\vartheta^{A}}=0
.

Lemma 4.1
.

\{\mathring{L},\mathring{\underline{L}},X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}\}
constitutes a null frame with respect to the
metric
g
, and admits the following identities:

\displaystyle g(\mathring{L},\mathring{L})=g(\mathring{\underline{L}},\mathring{\underline{L}})=g(\mathring{L},X_{A})=g(\mathring{\underline{L}},X_{A})=0,

(4.70)

\displaystyle g(\mathring{L},\mathring{\underline{L}})=-2\mu.

(4.71)

In addition,

\displaystyle\mathring{L}t=1,\quad\mathring{L}u=0,

(4.72)

\displaystyle\mathring{\underline{L}}t=\mu,\quad\mathring{\underline{L}}u=2.

(4.73)

And

\displaystyle g(\mathring{L},T)=-\mu,\quad g(T,T)=\mu^{2},

(4.74)

\displaystyle Tt=0,\quad Tu=1.

(4.75)

In the new coordinate
(s,u,\vartheta^{1},\vartheta^{2},\vartheta^{3})
defined by (
1.31
),
we will focus on the following subsets (see Figure 6):

Definition 4.1
.

\begin{split}&\Sigma_{s}^{u}:=\{(s^{\prime},u^{\prime},\vartheta):s^{\prime}=s,0\leq u^{\prime}\leq u\},\quad u\in[0,4\delta],\\
&C_{u}:=\{(s^{\prime},u^{\prime},\vartheta):s^{\prime}\geq t_{0},u^{\prime}=u\},\\
&C_{u}^{s}:=\{(s^{\prime},u^{\prime},\vartheta):t_{0}\leq s^{\prime}\leq s,u^{\prime}=u\},\\
&S_{s,u}:=\Sigma_{s}\cap C_{u},\\
&D^{s,u}:=\{(s^{\prime},u^{\prime},\vartheta):t_{0}\leq s^{\prime}<s,0\leq u^{\prime}\leq u\}.\end{split}

(4.76)

Figure 6. The indications of some domains

Next the following geometric notations will be used.

Definition 4.2
.

For the metric
g
on the spacetime,

•

\underline{g}=(g_{ij})
is defined as the induced metric of
g
on
\Sigma_{t}
, i.e.,
\underline{g}(U,V)=g(U,V)
for any tangent vectors
U
and
V
of
\Sigma_{t}
;

•

\not{\Pi}_{\alpha}^{\beta}:=\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}-\delta_{\alpha}^{0}\mathring{L}^{\beta}+\mathring{L}_{\alpha}\tilde{T}^{\beta}
is the
projection tensor field on
S_{s,u}
of type
(1,1)
, where
\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}
is Kronecker delta;

•

define
\not{\xi}:=\not{\Pi}\xi
as the tensor field on
S_{s,u}
for any
(m,n)
-type spacetime tensor field

\xi
, whose components are

\not{\xi}^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{m}}_{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{n}}:=(\not{\Pi}\xi)^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{m}}_{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{n}}=\not{\Pi}_{\beta_{1}}^{\beta_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots\not{\Pi}_{\beta_{n}}^{\beta_{n}^{\prime}}\not{\Pi}_{\alpha_{1}^{\prime}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\not{\Pi}_{\alpha_{m}^{\prime}}^{\alpha_{m}}\xi^{\alpha_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\alpha_{m}^{\prime}}_{\beta_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\beta_{n}^{\prime}},

in particular,
\not{g}=(\not{g}_{\alpha\beta})
is the induced metric of
g
on
S_{s,u}
;

•

(\not{g}^{AB})
is defined as the inverse matrix of
(\not{g}_{AB})
with
\not{g}_{AB}=g(X_{A},X_{B})
;

•

\mathscr{D}
and
\not{\nabla}
denote the Levi-Civita connection of
g
and
\not{g}
, respectively;

•

\Box_{g}:=g^{\alpha\beta}\mathscr{D}^{2}_{\alpha\beta}
,
\not{\triangle}:=\not{g}^{AB}\not{\nabla}^{2}_{AB}
;

•

define
\mathcal{L}_{V}\xi
as the Lie derivative of
\xi
with respect to
V
and
\not{\mathcal{L}}_{V}\xi:=\not{\Pi}(\mathcal{L}_{V}\xi)
for any tensor field
\xi
and vector
V
;

•

when
\xi
is a
(m,n)
-type spacetime tensor field, the square of its norm is defined as

|\xi|^{2}:=g_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots g_{\alpha_{m}\alpha_{m}^{\prime}}g^{\beta_{1}\beta_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots g^{\beta_{n}\beta_{n}^{\prime}}\xi_{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{n}}^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{m}}\xi_{\beta_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\beta_{n}^{\prime}}^{\alpha_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\alpha_{m}^{\prime}}.

In the null frame
\{\mathring{L},\mathring{\underline{L}},X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}\}
,
define
the second fundamental forms

\chi
and
\sigma
as

\chi_{AB}:=g(\mathscr{D}_{A}\mathring{L},X_{B}),\quad\sigma_{AB}:=g(\mathscr{D}_{A}\tilde{T},X_{B}),

(4.77)

where
\mathscr{D}_{A}
stands for
\mathscr{D}_{X_{A}}
.
Meanwhile, define the
one-form tensors

\zeta
and
\eta
as

\zeta_{A}:=g(\mathscr{D}_{A}\mathring{L},\tilde{T}),\quad\eta_{A}:=-g(\mathscr{D}_{A}T,\mathring{L}).

(4.78)

Then
\mu\zeta_{A}=-X_{A}\mu+\eta_{A}
.
For any vector field
V
, denote its associated
deformation tensor
by

{\vphantom{\pi}}^{(V)}\pi_{\alpha\beta}:=g(\mathscr{D}_{\alpha}V,\partial_{\beta})+g(\mathscr{D}_{\beta}V,\partial_{\alpha}).

(4.79)

On the initial hypersurface
\Sigma_{t_{0}}^{4\delta}
, it holds that
\tilde{T}^{i}=\displaystyle-\frac{x^{i}}{r}+O(\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}})
,

\mathring{L}^{0}=1
,
\mathring{L}^{i}=\displaystyle\frac{x^{i}}{r}+O(\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}})
and

\chi_{AB}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}\not{g}_{AB}+O(\delta^{1-\varepsilon_{0}})
.
Note that on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
,
r
is
t_{0}-u
. For
t\geq t_{0}
, one can define the “
error vectors
” as

\begin{split}&\check{L}^{0}:=0,\\
&\check{L}^{i}:=\mathring{L}^{i}-\frac{x^{i}}{\varrho},\\
&\check{T}^{i}:=\tilde{T}^{i}+\frac{x^{i}}{\varrho},\\
&\check{\chi}_{AB}:=\chi_{AB}-\frac{1}{\varrho}\not{g}_{AB},\end{split}

(4.80)

here and below
\varrho
is defined as
s-u
.

It is noted that
\vartheta=(\vartheta^{1},\vartheta^{2},\vartheta^{3})
are the coordinates on the sphere
S_{s,u}
.
Then in the new coordinate system
(s,u,\vartheta^{1},\vartheta^{2},\vartheta^{3})
,
one has
\mathring{L}=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}
. In addition, it follows from
(
4.75
) that
T=\frac{\partial}{\partial u}-\Xi
with
\Xi=\Xi^{A}X_{A}

for some smooth
\Xi^{A}
,
A=1,2,3
. Lemma 3.66 in
[
39
]
yields

Lemma 4.2
.

In domain
D^{s,u}
, the Jacobian determinant of the map
(s,u,\vartheta^{1},\vartheta^{2},\vartheta^{3})\rightarrow(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3},x^{4})
is

\det\frac{\partial(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3},x^{4})}{\partial(s,u,\vartheta^{1},\vartheta^{2},\vartheta^{3})}=\mu(\det\underline{g})^{-1/2}\sqrt{\det\not{g}},

(4.81)

where
\det\not{g}=\det(\not{g}_{AB})
.

Remark 4.1
.

It follows from (
4.81
) that if the metrics
\underline{g}
and
\not{g}
are regular, i.e.,
\det\underline{g}>0

and
\det\not{g}>0
, then the transformation of coordinates between
(s,u,\vartheta^{1},\vartheta^{2},\vartheta^{3})

and
(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3},x^{4})
makes sense
as long as
\mu>0
.

On
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, the standard rotation vector fields
\Omega_{ij}=x^{i}\partial_{j}-x^{j}\partial_{i}
are used
as tangent derivatives.
In order to project
\Omega_{ij}
on
S_{s,u}
, as in (3.39b) of
[
39
]
, one defines

R_{ij}:=\not{\Pi}\Omega_{ij},\quad\not{d}:=\not{\Pi}d

as the rotation vectorfields and the differential on
S_{s,u}
, respectively. Note that the explicit expression of
R_{ij}
is

R_{ij}=(\delta_{l}^{k}-g_{al}\tilde{T}^{a}\tilde{T}^{k}){\Omega_{ij}}^{l}\partial_{k}=\Omega_{ij}-g_{al}\tilde{T}^{a}{\Omega_{ij}}^{l}\tilde{T}.

(4.82)

For the sake of brevity, set

\upsilon_{ij}:=g_{al}\tilde{T}^{a}{\Omega_{ij}}^{l}=g_{kj}\check{T}^{k}x^{i}-g_{ki}\check{T}^{k}x^{j}-(g_{kj}-m_{kj})\frac{x^{k}x^{i}}{\varrho}+(g_{ki}-m_{ki})\frac{x^{k}x^{j}}{\varrho}.

(4.83)

Then

R_{ij}=\Omega_{ij}-\upsilon_{ij}\tilde{T}.

For the domains with
\mu>0
, the following integrations and norms
will be utilized repeatedly in subsequent sections.

Definition 4.3
.

For any continuous function
f
and tensor field
\xi
, define

\displaystyle\int_{S_{s,u}}f:=\int_{S_{s,u}}fd\nu_{\not{g}}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{3}}f(s,u,\vartheta)\sqrt{\det\not{g}(s,u,\vartheta)}d\vartheta,

\displaystyle\int_{C^{s}_{u}}f:=\int_{t_{0}}^{s}\int_{S_{\tau,u}}f(\tau,u,\vartheta)d\nu_{\not{g}}d\tau,

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{s}^{u}}f:=\int_{0}^{u}\int_{S_{s,u^{\prime}}}f(s,u^{\prime},\vartheta)d\nu_{\not{g}}du^{\prime},

\displaystyle\int_{D^{s,u}}f:=\int_{t_{0}}^{s}\int_{0}^{u}\int_{S_{\tau,u^{\prime}}}f(\tau,u^{\prime},\vartheta)d\nu_{\not{g}}du^{\prime}d\tau,

\displaystyle\|\xi\|_{L^{2}(S_{s,u})}^{2}:=\int_{S_{s,u}}|\xi|^{2},\quad\|\xi\|_{L^{2}(C^{s}_{u})}^{2}:=\int_{C^{s}_{u}}|\xi|^{2},

\displaystyle\|\xi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s}^{u})}^{2}:=\int_{\Sigma_{s}^{u}}|\xi|^{2},\quad\|\xi\|_{L^{2}(D^{s,u})}^{2}:=\int_{D^{s,u}}|\xi|^{2}.

Finally, the following contraction notations will be used.

Definition 4.4
.

If
\xi
is a
(0,2)
-type spacetime tensor,
\Lambda
is a 1-form,
U
and
V
are vector fields, then the contraction
of
\xi
with respect to
U
and
V
is defined as

\xi_{UV}:=\xi_{\alpha\beta}U^{\alpha}V^{\beta},

and the contraction of
\Lambda
with respect to
U
is

\Lambda_{U}:=\Lambda_{\alpha}U^{\alpha}.

Definition 4.5
.

If
\xi
is a
(0,2)
-type tensor on
S_{s,u}
, the trace of
\xi
can be defined as

\text{tr}\xi:=\not{g}^{AB}\xi_{AB}.

4.2.
Basic equalities in the null frames

In this subsection, we will derive some basic equalities in the frame
\{\mathring{L},\mathring{\underline{L}},X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}\}

or the frame
\{T,\mathring{\underline{L}},X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}\}
.

Let

\begin{split}&\displaystyle F_{\alpha\beta}:=\partial_{\phi}g_{\alpha\beta}\qquad\text{and}\qquad G_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}:=\partial_{\varphi_{\gamma}}g_{\alpha\beta}.\end{split}

(4.84)

For any vector fields
\displaystyle U=U^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}
and
\displaystyle V=V^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}
, set
F_{UV}=F_{\alpha\beta}U^{\alpha}V^{\beta}
and
G_{UV}^{\gamma}=G_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}U^{\alpha}V^{\beta}
as in Definition
4.4
.
In addition, define

(\mathcal{FG})_{UV}(W):=F_{UV}W\phi+G_{UV}^{\gamma}W\varphi_{\gamma},

(4.85)

where
U
,
V
,
W
are any vector fields.

We now derive the transport equation for
\mu
along
\mathring{L}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

\mu
satisfies

\begin{split}\mathring{L}\mu=-\frac{1}{2}\mu\big\{(\mathcal{FG})_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}(\mathring{L})+2(\mathcal{FG})_{\tilde{T}\mathring{L}}({\mathring{L}})\big\}+\frac{1}{2}F_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}T\phi+\frac{1}{2}G_{\mathring{L}\mathring{L}}^{\gamma}T\varphi_{\gamma}.\end{split}

(4.86)

Remark 4.2
.

As will be seen later, special attention is required to treat the terms containing
T\varphi_{\gamma}

since
T\varphi_{\gamma}
has the worse smallness or slower time-decay rate than the
ones of
Z\phi
and
\bar{Z}\varphi_{\gamma}
,
where
Z\in\{\mathring{L},T,X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}\}
and
\bar{Z}\in\{\mathring{L},X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}\}
.

Proof.

Note that
{\tilde{L}}=-g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\partial_{\beta}
